Um, well... I guess if I really think about it, it's probably been happening for a while? Like, even back when I was a kid. But I was a good kid, I promise! I wasn't, like, a troublemaker or anything.

Um, well... I really wish I could say I'm great at planning things out, but... I guess I do struggle with it a little bit. I mean, I try really hard to be organized, I promise! I have all these to-do lists and good intentions.

I really don't want you to think I'm just sitting there daydreaming on purpose or blowing off my work. It just sort of happens before I catch it. Is that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing that your mind wanders like this? Has this been happening since you were a kid, or is it something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess if I really think about it, it's probably been happening for a while? Like, even back when I was a kid. But I was a good kid, I promise! I wasn't, like, a troublemaker or anything. \n\nI just... um, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had really good ideas and was very creative. They liked me, I think. But they'd also mention that I, um, forgot to turn in my worksheets sometimes, or that I'd lose the instructions for an assignment. Or I'd just miss little details on things. \n\nMy parents were really great about it, though. They would just check my backpack every night to make sure I wasn't missing anything, and that usually fixed it. So it wasn't a huge problem! I just... I guess my brain has always worked a little bit like this. I hope that's not too unusual or bad? Does that help give you a better picture of things?\n\nDoctor: Besides your mind wandering, do you find it hard to sit still? Like, do you feel restless or fidgety, or do you have a lot of energy that's difficult to contain?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! I'm definitely not the hyper type. I mean, I can sit still just fine, I promise. I don't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. \n\nI might, um, tap my foot sometimes or play with my pen if I'm trying really hard to think, but I'm pretty sure everyone does that, right? It's just a little nervous habit, nothing major. I don't feel like I have all this pent-up energy or anything... I'm actually pretty calm most of the time. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering that right.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or having a conversation, do you find yourself interrupting other people, or do you sometimes blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really try my best to be polite and listen when other people are talking. I would hate to make anyone feel like I'm being rude or, um, talking over them. \n\nI mean, I guess if I get really, really excited about a topic I might talk a little fast? But I don't just blurt things out or interrupt people, I promise. I usually just keep my thoughts to myself until it's my turn to speak, or I just nod along. I'm actually a pretty good listener! \n\nIs that... I hope that's a good thing? I just really try not to be a bother or step on anyone's toes when we're talking.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your parents helped you stay organized in elementary school, and now you're having trouble with work submissions. How much do you think these attention issues are actually affecting your ability to do your job or school work? Are there real consequences, or is it more just feeling a bit embarrassed about the occasional mistake?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart or anything, because I'm trying really hard. But, um... I guess it is starting to have some real consequences. I'm so sorry to admit that. \n\nIt's just, you know, I've ended up submitting some work late recently, which I feel terrible about. And... um... I actually missed a couple of application deadlines that I really should have been on top of. It's not because I didn't care, I promise! I just... I lost track of the dates or got tangled up in the details. \n\nAnd, well, one of my mentors actually sat me down recently and told me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. That was really hard to hear, because I respect them so much and I want to do a good job. I just... I don't want people to think I'm lazy or irresponsible. I really am trying. Is it okay that I'm struggling with this a little bit? I hope it doesn't make me sound like a bad student.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing track of dates and getting tangled up in details. When you try to organize your time or plan out a project from start to finish, do you find that difficult, or does that come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really wish I could say I'm great at planning things out, but... I guess I do struggle with it a little bit. I mean, I try really hard to be organized, I promise! I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? Like, there are sort of half-started projects everywhere. I've got rent papers mixed up with gallery notes, and all these article PDFs saved, and sticky notes everywhere. It's not that I don't care, I just... I sit down to plan a project and I have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first. Everything feels equally important, you know? \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely chaotic. I do eventually get things done, mostly! It just takes me a little longer to sort through the mess. Is it normal to get a little overwhelmed by all the steps like that?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned these attention and organization challenges at work and in school. Do you also notice similar kinds of problems in your personal life—like keeping track of appointments, managing your finances, or staying on top of household tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I do have a little bit of trouble with that kind of stuff sometimes, too. But I really try to be responsible! I don't want you to think I'm just letting everything fall apart. \n\nIt's just that sometimes, I'll, um, I'll totally mean to do those normal adult things, you know? Like returning an email from a friend, or paying a bill, or just remembering to pick up groceries. I have every intention of doing it right then. But then... I don't know, days will go by, and I'll suddenly realize I never actually did it. It's like it just completely drops out of my mind. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, especially if someone is waiting on me. I promise I'm not trying to be careless or ignore my responsibilities. It just slips away sometimes. Is that... do a lot of people struggle with remembering those little everyday things? Um... wow, looking back... I guess I'm not really sure if it was happening everywhere. Does that answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: When you're working on these tasks or forms, do you find it hard to focus or concentrate, or do you feel like your mind is wandering? Or does it seem more like you're focused but just missing details when you review your work?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I wouldn't say I'm completely unfocused... I really do try to pay attention to what I'm doing, I promise. But, um, I guess my mind does wander a little bit sometimes? \n\nLike, if I’m trying to read or write at my desk, and there's a siren outside, or maybe just someone talking out in the hallway... or even if I just get a random thought about an exhibit idea... it can kind of pull me away. And, um, sometimes maybe twenty minutes will go by before I actually notice I'm not looking at my work anymore. \n\nBut I always get right back to it as soon as I realize! I really don't want you to think I'm just sitting there daydreaming on purpose or blowing off my work. It just sort of happens before I catch it. Is that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing that your mind wanders like this? Has this been happening since you were a kid, or is it something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess if I really think about it, it's probably been happening for a while? Like, even back when I was a kid. But I was a good kid, I promise! I wasn't, like, a troublemaker or anything. \n\nI just... um, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had really good ideas and was very creative. They liked me, I think. But they'd also mention that I, um, forgot to turn in my worksheets sometimes, or that I'd lose the instructions for an assignment. Or I'd just miss little details on things. \n\nMy parents were really great about it, though. They would just check my backpack every night to make sure I wasn't missing anything, and that usually fixed it. So it wasn't a huge problem! I just... I guess my brain has always worked a little bit like this. I hope that's not too unusual or bad? Does that help give you a better picture of things?\n\nDoctor: Besides your mind wandering, do you find it hard to sit still? Like, do you feel restless or fidgety, or do you have a lot of energy that's difficult to contain?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! I'm definitely not the hyper type. I mean, I can sit still just fine, I promise. I don't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. \n\nI might, um, tap my foot sometimes or play with my pen if I'm trying really hard to think, but I'm pretty sure everyone does that, right? It's just a little nervous habit, nothing major. I don't feel like I have all this pent-up energy or anything... I'm actually pretty calm most of the time. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering that right.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or having a conversation, do you find yourself interrupting other people, or do you sometimes blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really try my best to be polite and listen when other people are talking. I would hate to make anyone feel like I'm being rude or, um, talking over them. \n\nI mean, I guess if I get really, really excited about a topic I might talk a little fast? But I don't just blurt things out or interrupt people, I promise. I usually just keep my thoughts to myself until it's my turn to speak, or I just nod along. I'm actually a pretty good listener! \n\nIs that... I hope that's a good thing? I just really try not to be a bother or step on anyone's toes when we're talking.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your parents helped you stay organized in elementary school, and now you're having trouble with work submissions. How much do you think these attention issues are actually affecting your ability to do your job or school work? Are there real consequences, or is it more just feeling a bit embarrassed about the occasional mistake?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart or anything, because I'm trying really hard. But, um... I guess it is starting to have some real consequences. I'm so sorry to admit that. \n\nIt's just, you know, I've ended up submitting some work late recently, which I feel terrible about. And... um... I actually missed a couple of application deadlines that I really should have been on top of. It's not because I didn't care, I promise! I just... I lost track of the dates or got tangled up in the details. \n\nAnd, well, one of my mentors actually sat me down recently and told me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. That was really hard to hear, because I respect them so much and I want to do a good job. I just... I don't want people to think I'm lazy or irresponsible. I really am trying. Is it okay that I'm struggling with this a little bit? I hope it doesn't make me sound like a bad student.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing track of dates and getting tangled up in details. When you try to organize your time or plan out a project from start to finish, do you find that difficult, or does that come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really wish I could say I'm great at planning things out, but... I guess I do struggle with it a little bit. I mean, I try really hard to be organized, I promise! I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? Like, there are sort of half-started projects everywhere. I've got rent papers mixed up with gallery notes, and all these article PDFs saved, and sticky notes everywhere. It's not that I don't care, I just... I sit down to plan a project and I have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first. Everything feels equally important, you know? \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely chaotic. I do eventually get things done, mostly! It just takes me a little longer to sort through the mess. Is it normal to get a little overwhelmed by all the steps like that?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned these attention and organization challenges at work and in school. Do you also notice similar kinds of problems in your personal life—like keeping track of appointments, managing your finances, or staying on top of household tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I do have a little bit of trouble with that kind of stuff sometimes, too. But I really try to be responsible! I don't want you to think I'm just letting everything fall apart. \n\nIt's just that sometimes, I'll, um, I'll totally mean to do those normal adult things, you know? Like returning an email from a friend, or paying a bill, or just remembering to pick up groceries. I have every intention of doing it right then. But then... I don't know, days will go by, and I'll suddenly realize I never actually did it. It's like it just completely drops out of my mind. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, especially if someone is waiting on me. I promise I'm not trying to be careless or ignore my responsibilities. It just slips away sometimes. Is that... do a lot of people struggle with remembering those little everyday things? I really hope it doesn't make me seem incapable.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your teachers noted these things back in elementary school. Looking back, do you remember if you had similar kinds of trouble—like losing things, forgetting assignments, or having a hard time paying attention—across different settings? Like, was it happening at home, at school, and maybe even during activities or sports?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, looking back... I guess I'm not really sure if it was happening everywhere. I mean, my parents definitely helped me keep my backpack organized at home so I wouldn't lose things before school, which was super nice of them! But... I don't really remember if I was forgetting things at soccer practice or, like, at my friends' houses. \n\nI was a pretty good kid, I promise! I wasn't, you know, constantly getting in trouble for not paying attention or anything. I always tried to listen when people were talking to me. I just... I don't really have a clear memory of it being a huge problem across the board. Is it okay if I don't remember all the details from back then? I really hope I'm being helpful enough.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to work on something that requires a lot of focus, like reading or writing, do you ever feel like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel antsy even though you're able to physically stay seated?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I'm actually pretty good at staying in my seat, I promise! I definitely don't bounce off the walls or pace around or anything like that. \n\nI always try to be really respectful, especially if I'm, you know, trying to read in a library or if I'm around other people, so I really don't want to be disruptive. Physically, I can sit at my desk for a really long time without feeling antsy at all. My mind might drift off a little bit, like I mentioned, but my body stays put. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I just... yeah, I don't really have that urge to get up and move around when I'm supposed to be working.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if your teachers or parents noticed the same kinds of things—like losing assignments, forgetting instructions, or having trouble paying attention—or was it mainly just something your teachers mentioned on report cards?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really did try my best to do what I was supposed to. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nAs for the exact grade... um, I think it was pretty early on. Like, maybe first or second grade? Basically, even in early elementary school is when they started writing those notes. Like I mentioned, they'd always say I had good ideas, but that I missed little details or lost my instructions. And if my parents hadn't been checking my backpack every single night, I probably would have forgotten to turn in a lot more of those worksheets. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't remember the exact year? Am I giving you enough detail?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I always wanted to do a good job. \n\nActually, my teachers were really nice about it. They would always write that I had really good ideas, which I appreciated so much. It's just... they would also mention that I forgot to turn in my worksheets sometimes, or that I'd lose instructions or miss little details. \n\nBut my parents were so great about it. They would check my backpack every single night just to make sure everything was in order and that I hadn't lost anything. I really did try my best to do what I was supposed to. I just really try not to be a bother or step on anyone's toes when we're talking.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your parents helped you stay organized in elementary school, and now you're having trouble with work submissions. How much do you think these attention issues are actually affecting your ability to do your job or school work? Are there real consequences, or is it more just feeling a bit embarrassed about the occasional mistake?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart or anything, because I'm trying really hard. But, um... I guess it is starting to have some real consequences. I'm so sorry to admit that. \n\nIt's just, you know, I've ended up submitting some work late recently, which I feel terrible about. And... um... I actually missed a couple of application deadlines that I really should have been on top of. It's not because I didn't care, I promise! I just... I lost track of the dates or got tangled up in the details. \n\nAnd, well, one of my mentors actually sat me down recently and told me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. That was really hard to hear, because I respect them so much and I want to do a good job. I just... I don't want people to think I'm lazy or irresponsible. I really am trying. Is it okay that I'm struggling with this a little bit? I hope it doesn't make me sound like a bad student.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing track of dates and getting tangled up in details. When you try to organize your time or plan out a project from start to finish, do you find that difficult, or does that come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? It's not that I don't care, I just... I sit down to plan a project and I have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first. Everything feels equally important, you know? \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely chaotic. I do eventually get things done, mostly! It just takes me a little longer to sort through the mess. Do you also notice similar kinds of problems in your personal life—like keeping track of appointments, managing your finances, or staying on top of household tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I don't want you to think I'm just letting everything fall apart. \n\nIt's just that sometimes, I'll, um, I'll totally mean to do those normal adult things, you know? Like returning an email from a friend, or paying a bill, or just remembering to pick up groceries. I have every intention of doing it right then. But then... I don't know, days will go by, and I'll suddenly realize I never actually did it. It's like it just completely drops out of my mind. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, especially if someone is waiting on me. I'm actually pretty good at staying in my seat, I promise! I'm so sorry to admit that. \n\nIt's just, you know, I've ended up submitting some work late recently, which I feel terrible about. And... um... I actually missed a couple of application deadlines that I really should have been on top of. It's not because I didn't care, I promise! I just... I lost track of the dates or got tangled up in the details. \n\nAnd, well, one of my mentors actually sat me down recently and told me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. That was really hard to hear, because I respect them so much and I want to do a good job. I just... I don't want people to think I'm lazy or irresponsible. I really am trying. Is it okay that I'm struggling with this a little bit? I hope it doesn't make me sound like a bad student.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing track of dates and getting tangled up in details. When you try to organize your time or plan out a project from start to finish, do you find that difficult, or does that come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really wish I could say I'm great at planning things out, but... I guess I do struggle with it a little bit. I mean, I try really hard to be organized, I promise! I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? Like, there are sort of half-started projects everywhere. I've got rent papers mixed up with gallery notes, and all these article PDFs saved, and sticky notes everywhere. It's not that I don't care, I just... I sit down to plan a project and I have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first. It just takes me a little longer to sort through the mess. Do you also notice similar kinds of problems in your personal life—like keeping track of appointments, managing your finances, or staying on top of household tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I do have a little bit of trouble with that kind of stuff sometimes, too. But I really try to be responsible! I don't want you to think I'm just letting everything fall apart. \n\nIt's just that sometimes, I'll, um, I'll totally mean to do those normal adult things, you know? Like returning an email from a friend, or paying a bill, or just remembering to pick up groceries. I have every intention of doing it right then. But then... I don't know, days will go by, and I'll suddenly realize I never actually did it. It's like it just completely drops out of my mind. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, especially if someone is waiting on me. I promise I'm not trying to be careless or ignore my responsibilities. It just slips away sometimes. Is that... do a lot of people struggle with remembering those little everyday things? I really hope it doesn't make me seem incapable.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your teachers noted these things back in elementary school. Looking back, do you remember if you had similar kinds of trouble—like losing things, forgetting assignments, or having a hard time paying attention—across different settings? Like, was it happening at home, at school, and maybe even during activities or sports?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, looking back... I guess I'm not really sure if it was happening everywhere. I mean, my parents definitely helped me keep my backpack organized at home so I wouldn't lose things before school, which was super nice of them! But... I don't really remember if I was forgetting things at soccer practice or, like, at my friends' houses. \n\nI was a pretty good kid, I promise! I wasn't, you know, constantly getting in trouble for not paying attention or anything. I always tried to listen when people were talking to me. I just... I don't really have a clear memory of it being a huge problem across the board. Is it okay if I don't remember all the details from back then? I really hope I'm being helpful enough.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to work on something that requires a lot of focus, like reading or writing, do you ever feel like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel antsy even though you're able to physically stay seated?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I'm actually pretty good at staying in my seat, I promise! I just... yeah, I don't really have that urge to get up and move around when I'm supposed to be working.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if your teachers or parents noticed the same kinds of things—like losing assignments, forgetting instructions, or having trouble paying attention—or was it mainly just something your teachers mentioned on report cards?\n\nPatient: Um, well... Like, do you feel restless or fidgety, or do you have a lot of energy that's difficult to contain?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! I'm definitely not the hyper type. I mean, I can sit still just fine, I promise. I don't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. \n\nI might, um, tap my foot sometimes or play with my pen if I'm trying really hard to think, but I'm pretty sure everyone does that, right? It's just a little nervous habit, nothing major. I don't feel like I have all this pent-up energy or anything... I'm actually pretty calm most of the time. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering that right.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or having a conversation, do you find yourself interrupting other people, or do you sometimes blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really try my best to be polite and listen when other people are talking. I would hate to make anyone feel like I'm being rude or, um, talking over them. \n\nI mean, I guess if I get really, really excited about a topic I might talk a little fast? I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? But, um, I guess my mind does wander a little bit sometimes? \n\nLike, if I’m trying to read or write at my desk, and there's a siren outside, or maybe just someone talking out in the hallway... or even if I just get a random thought about an exhibit idea... it can kind of pull me away. And, um, sometimes maybe twenty minutes will go by before I actually notice I'm not looking at my work anymore. \n\nBut I always get right back to it as soon as I realize! I really don't want you to think I'm just sitting there daydreaming on purpose or blowing off my work. It just sort of happens before I catch it. Is that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing that your mind wanders like this? Has this been happening since you were a kid, or is it something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess if I really think about it, it's probably been happening for a while? Like, even back when I was a kid. But I was a good kid, I promise! I wasn't, like, a troublemaker or anything. \n\nI just... um, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had really good ideas and was very creative. They liked me, I think. But they'd also mention that I, um, forgot to turn in my worksheets sometimes, or that I'd lose the instructions for an assignment. Or I'd just miss little details on things. \n\nMy parents were really great about it, though. They would just check my backpack every night to make sure I wasn't missing anything, and that usually fixed it. So it wasn't a huge problem! I just... I guess my brain has always worked a little bit like this. I hope that's not too unusual or bad? Does that help give you a better picture of things?\n\nDoctor: Besides your mind wandering, do you find it hard to sit still? Like, do you feel restless or fidgety, or do you have a lot of energy that's difficult to contain?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all! I'm definitely not the hyper type. I mean, I can sit still just fine, I promise. I don't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. \n\nI might, um, tap my foot sometimes or play with my pen if I'm trying really hard to think, but I'm pretty sure everyone does that, right? It's just a little nervous habit, nothing major. I don't feel like I have all this pent-up energy or anything... I'm actually pretty calm most of the time. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering that right.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or having a conversation, do you find yourself interrupting other people, or do you sometimes blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really try my best to be polite and listen when other people are talking. I would hate to make anyone feel like I'm being rude or, um, talking over them. \n\nI mean, I guess if I get really, really excited about a topic I might talk a little fast? But I don't just blurt things out or interrupt people, I promise. I usually just keep my thoughts to myself until it's my turn to speak, or I just nod along. I'm actually a pretty good listener! \n\nIs that... I hope that's a good thing? I just really try not to be a bother or step on anyone's toes when we're talking.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your parents helped you stay organized in elementary school, and now you're having trouble with work submissions. How much do you think these attention issues are actually affecting your ability to do your job or school work? Are there real consequences, or is it more just feeling a bit embarrassed about the occasional mistake?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart or anything, because I'm trying really hard. But, um... I guess it is starting to have some real consequences. I'm so sorry to admit that. \n\nIt's just, you know, I've ended up submitting some work late recently, which I feel terrible about. And... um... I actually missed a couple of application deadlines that I really should have been on top of. It's not because I didn't care, I promise! I just... I lost track of the dates or got tangled up in the details. \n\nAnd, well, one of my mentors actually sat me down recently and told me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. That was really hard to hear, because I respect them so much and I want to do a good job. I just... I don't want people to think I'm lazy or irresponsible. I really am trying. Is it okay that I'm struggling with this a little bit? I hope it doesn't make me sound like a bad student.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing track of dates and getting tangled up in details. When you try to organize your time or plan out a project from start to finish, do you find that difficult, or does that come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really wish I could say I'm great at planning things out, but... I guess I do struggle with it a little bit. I mean, I try really hard to be organized, I promise! I have all these to-do lists and good intentions. \n\nIt's just... if you looked at my apartment right now, or even my laptop, it might look a tiny bit messy? Like, there are sort of half-started projects everywhere. I've got rent papers mixed up with gallery notes, and all these article PDFs saved, and sticky notes everywhere. It's not that I don't care, I just... I sit down to plan a project and I have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first. Everything feels equally important, you know? \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely chaotic. I do eventually get things done, mostly! It just takes me a little longer to sort through the mess. Is it normal to get a little overwhelmed by all the steps like that?\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned these attention and organization challenges at work and in school. Do you also notice similar kinds of problems in your personal life—like keeping track of appointments, managing your finances, or staying on top of household tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I do have a little bit of trouble with that kind of stuff sometimes, too. But I really try to be responsible! I don't want you to think I'm just letting everything fall apart. \n\nIt's just that sometimes, I'll, um, I'll totally mean to do those normal adult things, you know? Like returning an email from a friend, or paying a bill, or just remembering to pick up groceries. I have every intention of doing it right then. But then... I don't know, days will go by, and I'll suddenly realize I never actually did it. It's like it just completely drops out of my mind. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, especially if someone is waiting on me. I promise I'm not trying to be careless or ignore my responsibilities. It just slips away sometimes. Is that... do a lot of people struggle with remembering those little everyday things? I really hope it doesn't make me seem incapable.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your teachers noted these things back in elementary school. Looking back, do you remember if you had similar kinds of trouble—like losing things, forgetting assignments, or having a hard time paying attention—across different settings? Like, was it happening at home, at school, and maybe even during activities or sports?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, looking back... I guess I'm not really sure if it was happening everywhere. I mean, my parents definitely helped me keep my backpack organized at home so I wouldn't lose things before school, which was super nice of them! But... I don't really remember if I was forgetting things at soccer practice or, like, at my friends' houses. \n\nI was a pretty good kid, I promise! I wasn't, you know, constantly getting in trouble for not paying attention or anything. I always tried to listen when people were talking to me. I just... I don't really have a clear memory of it being a huge problem across the board. Is it okay if I don't remember all the details from back then? I really hope I'm being helpful enough.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to work on something that requires a lot of focus, like reading or writing, do you ever feel like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel antsy even though you're able to physically stay seated?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I'm actually pretty good at staying in my seat, I promise! I definitely don't bounce off the walls or pace around or anything like that. \n\nI always try to be really respectful, especially if I'm, you know, trying to read in a library or if I'm around other people, so I really don't want to be disruptive. Physically, I can sit at my desk for a really long time without feeling antsy at all. My mind might drift off a little bit, like I mentioned, but my body stays put. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I just... yeah, I don't really have that urge to get up and move around when I'm supposed to be working.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if your teachers or parents noticed the same kinds of things—like losing assignments, forgetting instructions, or having trouble paying attention—or was it mainly just something your teachers mentioned on report cards?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess both my teachers and my parents noticed it. But I really want to emphasize that I wasn't trying to be a bad student! I always wanted to do a good job. \n\nActually, my teachers were really nice about it. They would always write that I had really good ideas, which I appreciated so much. It's just... they would also mention that I forgot to turn in my worksheets sometimes, or that I'd lose instructions or miss little details. \n\nBut my parents were so great about it. They would check my backpack every single night just to make sure everything was in order and that I hadn't lost anything. So it really wasn't a huge disaster or anything, because they helped me stay on top of it! I hope needing that extra help doesn't make me sound like I was careless. I really did try my best to do what I was supposed to. Is it pretty normal for kids to need their parents to check their bags like that?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, around what grade do you think your teachers first started mentioning these things about forgetting assignments or losing instructions on their report cards?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, I know I was just talking about those report cards—sorry if I'm repeating myself! I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nAs for the exact grade... um, I think it was pretty early on. Like, maybe first or second grade? Basically, even in early elementary school is when they started writing those notes. Like I mentioned, they'd always say I had good ideas, but that I missed little details or lost my instructions. And if my parents hadn't been checking my backpack every single night, I probably would have forgotten to turn in a lot more of those worksheets. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't remember the exact year? I just know it was pretty much from the beginning of grade school. But my teachers were always really sweet about how they phrased it, I promise! Am I giving you enough detail?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your parents helped you stay organized at home, and your teachers noticed things at school. But thinking about other situations—like when you were with friends, at extracurricular activities, or just doing things on your own—do you remember having similar problems with losing things or forgetting what you were supposed to do in those settings too?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of school? I don't really think so... I mean, I hope not! I was always trying really hard to be a good friend and, you know, be responsible when I was at soccer or whatever. \n\nI guess... maybe I'd forget a shin guard or a water bottle every now and then? But I feel like all kids do that, right? It wasn't like I was constantly ruining things or letting people down. I really tried to pay attention when coaches were talking, and I always wanted to be helpful. I don't think it was a big issue outside of the classroom... at least, I hope nobody thought I was being a flake or anything. Does that make sense? I just really didn't want to be a burden on anyone.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or coaches ever mention to you that you were forgetting things or having trouble paying attention during activities outside of school, or was it really just your teachers at school who brought those things up?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I really don't think they ever brought it up. Like, my coaches never pulled me aside or anything to say I wasn't paying attention. I mean, I always tried to be super respectful when they were talking, you know? I really wanted to be a good team player and not cause any trouble. \n\nAnd my parents... well, they were so focused on helping me with my backpack and my schoolwork, but I don't remember them ever sitting me down to say I was, like, forgetting things at home or during my activities. It really was mostly just my teachers mentioning the assignments and the worksheets. \n\nI hope that's okay? Like, I just really don't want it to sound like I was ignoring people or being a difficult kid everywhere I went. I really did try to be good and listen to adults! Does that answer what you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, besides what was happening at school with your teachers and your parents checking your backpack at home, do you remember ever losing or forgetting things when you were at your friends' houses, or during soccer practice, or at other places outside of your home and school?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, I know you asked something similar just a minute ago—I'm so sorry if I'm making you repeat yourself! I really want to be helpful and give you the right information. \n\nBut, um, honestly? No, I don't really remember losing things at my friends' houses or at soccer practice. Like I said, maybe a water bottle here or there, but nothing major. I always tried really hard to be responsible and keep track of my stuff when I was out. I never wanted to be that kid who left a mess or lost someone else's toy, you know? I really tried to be careful. \n\nSo, yeah, it was pretty much just the school stuff. I hope that's the kind of answer you need? I just really don't want you to think I was completely careless everywhere I went. I did try my best!\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did your parents ever express concern to you about forgetting things, losing things, or having trouble paying attention at home—like when you were doing chores, homework, or just spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Um, I know we kind of touched on this, and I'm really sorry if I'm not giving you exactly what you need... but no, not really. \n\nLike, with homework, they were definitely involved, checking my backpack and making sure things didn't get lost between school and home. But they never sat me down and said they were worried about me paying attention around the house or doing my chores. I always tried to do what they asked right away because I didn't want them to get upset or think I was being lazy. I really wanted to be a good son and help out, you know? \n\nSo, I don't think they were ever really concerned about me at home. It was just the school stuff. Is that... is that okay? I hope I'm making sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
